My sister used to work in an office with those same lights and she had to get special glasses just to look at her computer screen. She works in real estate now, doing much better. But yeah, the sweets... I buy them, and then I just eat them without even tasting them, really. It's like I'm not even there.\n\nDoctor: When you find yourself buying and eating those sweets, do you feel like you're not in control of how much you're eating, like you can't stop once you start?\n\nPatient: No, I wouldn't really say it's like that. I don't feel out of control, exactly. It's not like I can't stop. Speaking of stopping, my ex-husband tried to stop smoking a few years back. He used those little patches? They left these awful red welts all over his shoulders. He said they itched terribly. We had to go see a dermatologist, Dr. Evans, who is just the nicest man. He has this beautiful saltwater aquarium right in his waiting room. I could sit and watch those little yellow fish all day long, it's so relaxing. Much better than staring at old magazines from three years ago, you know? They always have those ancient issues of Better Homes and Gardens. \n\nBut anyway... no, with the muffins and things, I could stop if I really wanted to. I just kind of eat them because they're sitting right there on the kitchen counter. I just don't really think about it while I'm doing it.\n\nDoctor: After you eat those sweets, do you feel guilty or upset with yourself about it?\n\nPatient: Guilty? No, not really. I don't get upset about it. It's just... they're just muffins, you know? Though, speaking of getting upset, my neighbor Sarah—the one with the celery juicer—she got so upset the other day because the neighborhood association sent her a letter about her garden hose. They said it was an \"eyesore\" because she left it coiled up next to the porch instead of hiding it behind the bushes. Can you believe that? An eyesore! She was practically in tears over it. I told her, I said, \"Sarah, it's a green hose on green grass, who is even looking at it?\" But she went out and bought this fancy decorative pot just to hide the hose in. It looks like a giant copper cauldron. Every time I look out my kitchen window now, I feel like she's brewing potions over there. \n\nBut no, I don't feel guilty about the sweets. I just eat them and then throw the boxes away. They do take up a lot of room in the recycling bin, though.\n\nDoctor: When you eat those sweets on these days, would you say you're eating a larger amount than most people would eat in that same amount of time?\n\nPatient: Well, I don't know if it's more than most people. I mean, what does a normal person eat, anyway? It's usually just a couple of those big muffins, maybe a sleeve of cookies. It doesn't seem like a mountain of food or anything. Speaking of mountains, did I ever tell you about the time my cousin took a trip to the Rockies? He sent back these postcards that were just stunning. Snow on the peaks in July! He said the air up there is so thin you get winded just walking to the car. He brought me back this little snow globe with a moose inside, but the water leaked out of it on the flight back. It's sitting on my dresser right now, just a dry plastic moose looking confused. It always makes me think of those poor animals in the zoo when it gets too hot. \n\nBut yeah, the sweets... I don't think it's a huge amount. Just whatever's in the box, really.\n\nDoctor: When you're eating those sweets, do you find yourself eating much more quickly than usual?\n\nPatient: Quickly? No, not really. I just eat them at a normal speed, I suppose. I don't see the point in rushing. \n\nEveryone is in such a terrible hurry these days, don't you think? Like our regular mail carrier, Gary. He practically sprints from his little truck up to the porches. Just last week, he was going so fast he tripped right over the garden gnome I have sitting by the bottom step—the one holding the little solar lantern? He knocked it clean over and chipped its red hat. I felt awful for him, but honestly, if he would just slow down a bit... He dropped half my mail right into the rhododendron bush. I was fishing out damp grocery store coupons for three days because it rained that Tuesday. The blue ink got all over my fingers, it was a mess to wash off. \n\nBut anyway... no, I'm not racing anybody to finish a cookie or a muffin. I just chew them normally.\n\nDoctor: Do you usually eat those sweets even when you're not physically hungry?\n\nPatient: Hungry? I don't know, I suppose I must be at least a little hungry when I eat them. I don't really pay much attention to my stomach rumbling or anything like that. \n\nBut speaking of being hungry, you know how they always tell you never to go grocery shopping on an empty stomach? Well, they are absolutely right about that. I went to the store right before lunch on Thursday, and I ended up buying three jars of pickled asparagus. I do not even like asparagus! But the jars just looked so tall and elegant sitting there on the shelf under those bright lights. The cashier, a young kid with a name tag that said Kevin, had to call over a manager because one of the jars would not scan. We stood there for ten minutes while the manager fiddled with the register tape. The line behind me was getting so long, people were shifting from foot to foot, and I felt just terrible. I ended up giving two of the jars to Sarah next door. She said she might try blending them in that juicer of hers. Blended pickled asparagus... it sounds like pond water, doesn't it? \n\nAnyway, what was I saying? Oh, right. I guess I just eat the muffins if I feel like having a snack, so I must be a little hungry for them.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself eating until you feel uncomfortably full when you have these episodes with the sweets?\n\nPatient: Uncomfortably full? No, I don't think so. I mean, I might feel a little full, like you do after a meal, but not sick or anything. I don't really gorge myself until I can't move. \n\nMy uncle Arthur used to do that, though, especially at Thanksgiving. He would eat so much turkey and stuffing that he'd have to unbutton the top of his trousers right there at the table. He always sat in this big recliner we had in the den afterward, and within ten minutes, he'd be snoring so loudly you could hear it in the kitchen over the sound of the dishwasher. My aunt would get so embarrassed, she'd try to wake him up by dropping a magazine on the floor near his head, but he'd just snort and go right back to sleep. They had this beautiful old house with a wraparound porch, and in the summer, they'd sit out there on a porch swing that squeaked every time it went back and forth. I always meant to bring over some WD-40 for that squeak, but I never remembered. I keep a can of it under my kitchen sink, actually, right next to the glass cleaner. You never know when a hinge is going to start acting up. \n\nBut no, I don't eat until I'm bursting. Just until I'm done, I guess.\n\nDoctor: Can you think of a time when you were eating those sweets—was there ever a point where you ate a lot in a short amount of time, like within an hour or two, even if it wasn’t a huge amount overall?\n\nPatient: An hour or two? No, I don't think I've ever just sat there and eaten a whole bunch of them in a short time like that. I don't really watch the clock when I'm having a snack. \n\nAlthough, speaking of clocks, I have this beautiful antique grandfather clock in my front hallway. It belonged to my grandmother, actually. It chimes on the hour, but lately, it’s been running about fifteen minutes fast. I tried to adjust the pendulum myself last week, but the little brass weight is so finicky. I ended up calling a repairman, an older gentleman named Mr. Henderson. He came over on Tuesday and spent three hours just looking at the gears with this tiny little magnifying glass attached to his glasses. He smelled very strongly of peppermint and pipe tobacco, which completely reminded me of my old high school history teacher, Mr. Davies. He used to smoke a pipe right outside the classroom window before the bell rang. Can you imagine a teacher doing that these days? The school board would have an absolute fit! They are so strict now about everything. \n\nBut anyway... what were we talking about? Oh, the sweets. No, I don't eat a lot of them all at once. Just one or two with my tea, usually.\n\nDoctor: After you eat those sweets, do you ever make yourself vomit, take laxatives, or exercise a lot to try to balance it out?\n\nPatient: Vomit? Oh, goodness, no. I could never do that. Just the thought of it makes my stomach turn. And I definitely don't exercise a lot. I mean, I walk to the mailbox, but that's about it. \n\nYou know, speaking of the mailbox, my mail carrier, Diane, she just started taking these aerobics classes at the community center down the road. She was telling me all about it on Wednesday when she dropped off a package for me. It was a new set of those microfiber dish towels I ordered from a catalog. Anyway, Diane said the instructor at this class makes them do these things called burpees? It sounded absolutely exhausting. She was wearing these bright neon pink sneakers, too, the kind that practically glow in the dark. I haven't seen shoes like that since the eighties. I remember I had a pair of leg warmers that were exactly that color. I wore them to a jazzercise class I took with my friend Linda back in 1985. Linda was always trying the newest fitness fads. She even bought one of those stationary bikes with the big fan wheel on the front, but she ended up just using it to hang her damp laundry on in the winter. It actually worked quite well for drying sweaters without stretching them out. \n\nBut no, I don't do anything like that after I have a muffin. I just eat it and go about my day.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever eat those sweets by yourself when no one else is around, or try to hide that you’ve eaten them from others?\n\nPatient: Hide them? No, I don't really hide them. I mean, I live alone, so there's not really anyone here to hide them from anyway. It's just me and the house. \n\nThough, my neighbor, Mrs. Gable, she's always popping over unannounced. She has this habit of just walking right through the back door if it's unlocked. She did it just last week, actually, to bring over some tomatoes from her garden. They were those big, ugly heirloom ones, but they tasted wonderful. Anyway, she startled me so badly I nearly dropped my teacup. She’s a sweet woman, but she talks a mile a minute, mostly about her grandson who’s away at college in Ohio. He's studying to be an engineer, I think. Or maybe an architect? I always get those two mixed up. He used to mow my lawn in the summers before he left. He did a terrible job around the edges, always missed the spot near the rhododendron bush, but I never had the heart to tell him. It’s hard to find reliable help these days. I tried looking in the local paper for a new lawn service, but the print is so small now, I can barely read it even with my reading glasses. I think I need a new prescription. \n\nBut no, I don't hide my sweets. If I want a cookie, I just have one.\n\nDoctor: Can you tell me if there are times when you eat a lot in a short amount of time, like over the course of a few hours, even if it doesn’t feel like you’re out of control while you're doing it?\n\nPatient: No, I really don't think so. I don't sit there for hours just eating and eating. I might have a little something, but not a lot all at once. \n\nAlthough, speaking of eating for hours, my cousin Barbara used to host these Thanksgiving dinners that felt like they went on for days. She lived in this huge Victorian house out in the country, and she would cook for a week beforehand. She always made this sweet potato casserole with the little marshmallows on top, but she would burn them just a tiny bit, which I actually prefer. Anyway, one year, her husband, Frank, decided he was going to deep-fry the turkey in the driveway. He bought this massive contraption, it looked like an oil drum. Well, he didn't measure the oil right or something, and the whole thing bubbled over and caught fire! It was a disaster. The fire department had to come, and the big red truck got stuck in the mud at the end of her driveway because it had rained for three days straight before that. The firemen were very nice about it, though. One of them, a young man with bright red hair, even stayed and had a plate of the sweet potatoes after they put the fire out. He said it reminded him of his grandmother's cooking. I haven't seen Barbara in years, though. We had a falling out over a quilt that belonged to our aunt. It's silly, really, how families fight over things like that. \n\nBut what were you asking? Oh, eating a lot in a few hours. No, I don't do that. Just a normal amount, I'd say.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever eaten a lot of food when you were alone because you felt embarrassed about how much you were eating or what you were eating?\n\nPatient: Embarrassed? No, I don't think I've ever felt embarrassed about eating. Like I said, I live alone, so it's not like there's anyone here to judge me anyway. If I want a piece of cake, I just have a piece of cake. \n\nYou know, talking about cake, the bakery down on Main Street—the one with the green awning, not the one by the post office—they make the most incredible lemon pound cake. I used to go there every Tuesday morning with my friend Clara. We’d sit in the window booth and watch the people walk by. Clara always wore these big, floppy hats, even in the dead of winter. She said they protected her skin, but I think she just liked the attention. She had a hat for every occasion. I remember she wore a bright yellow one with a sunflower on it to a funeral once. It was her great-uncle's funeral, and he loved gardening, so she said it was a tribute, but I think some of the other relatives were a bit put off by it. They were a very traditional family, the kind that only wore black and whispered the whole time. I always preferred a celebration of life, myself. When my cat, Mr. Whiskers, passed away, I invited a few neighbors over and we shared stories about him. He was a tabby, very independent, but he loved to sleep on top of the refrigerator. \n\nBut anyway, no, I don't hide my food or feel embarrassed about it. I just eat what I want when I want it.\n\nDoctor: Can you tell me if there are times when you eat a much larger amount of food than you would normally eat in one sitting, even if it doesn’t feel like a huge amount at the time?\n\nPatient: No, I wouldn't say I eat a much larger amount than normal, really. I just have my regular meals, maybe a snack here and there. I don't really have a big appetite anyway. \n\nMy appetite hasn't been the same since they closed down that diner on 4th Street. The Starlight Diner, it was called. They had these red vinyl booths that would stick to the back of your legs in the summer if you wore shorts. I used to go there for meatloaf on Thursdays. The waitress, Brenda, she always knew exactly how much gravy I liked. She had this big hair, you know, the kind they teased up real high in the eighties, and she always wore this bright pink lipstick that would leave a mark on the coffee cups. She told me once she bought it in bulk from some catalog because they were going to discontinue the color. \"Flamingo Sunrise,\" I think it was called. Or maybe \"Coral Sunset\"? Anyway, she had a daughter who was a competitive ice skater. She was always showing me pictures of her in these little sequined outfits. I think she made it to the regionals one year, but then she twisted her ankle doing a jump. It's a shame, really, she was very talented. I used to ice skate when I was a girl, out on the pond behind my uncle's farm, but the ice was never very smooth. You really had to watch out for the bumps or you'd go flying. \n\nBut no, I don't eat huge amounts of food in one sitting. Just my normal portions.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. The Starlight Diner, it was called. They had these red vinyl booths that would stick to the back of your legs in the summer if you wore shorts. I used to go there for meatloaf on Thursdays. The waitress, Brenda, she always knew exactly how much gravy I liked. She had this big hair, you know, the kind they teased up real high in the eighties, and she always wore this bright pink lipstick that would leave a mark on the coffee cups. She told me once she bought it in bulk from some catalog because they were going to discontinue the color. \"Flamingo Sunrise,\" I think it was called. Or maybe \"Coral Sunset\"? Anyway, she had a daughter who was a competitive ice skater. She was always showing me pictures of her in these little sequined outfits. I think she made it to the regionals one year, but then she twisted her ankle doing a jump. It's a shame, really, she was very talented. I used to ice skate when I was a girl, out on the pond behind my uncle's farm, but the ice was never very smooth. You really had to watch out for the bumps or you'd go flying. \n\nBut no, I don't eat huge amounts of food in one sitting. Just my normal portions.\n\n[Last Question]\nDo you ever eat alone because you're worried others might judge what or how much you're eating?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Female / 50 / not_in_workforce / high_school\n- severity: Mild\n\n[Targeted Info]\nNone\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Tangent patient.\nA patient who goes off on tangents may\n1) begin answering the doctor's question but quickly shift to an unrelated topic,\n2) frequently add irrelevant personal anecdotes or experiences while answering the question, and\n3) have difficulty remaining focused on the doctor's question, potentially requiring the doctor to repeat the question or redirect the conversation to obtain clear clinical information.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
No, I don't really worry about people judging me. I mostly eat alone anyway, but that's just because I live by myself. It's not because I'm hiding anything. 

Speaking of eating alone, I used to have this little parakeet named Pip. He was bright blue, like a summer sky, and he would sit on my shoulder while I ate my breakfast. He loved toast crumbs. I got him at a pet store that used to be over by the old mall. That mall is completely empty now, just a big concrete eyesore. They were supposed to turn it into an indoor water park or something, but the company ran out of money. It's a shame, really, because the town could use something fun for the kids. When I was a kid, we used to go to the roller rink on Friday nights. They played disco music and had a disco ball that scattered little squares of light all over the floor. I had these white skates with pink pom-poms on the toes. I thought I was just the bee's knees. 

But what were we talking about? Oh, eating alone. No, I don't hide from people to eat. I just eat my meals.

From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n15\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF50.2, F50.9\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. I'm Dr. [Name]. Can you tell me what brings you in today?\n\nPatient: Hi, Doctor. Well, I guess I came in because... my eating has just gotten all turned around. It's been going on for months now. Like, some days I just sit and pick at a piece of dry toast and have some coffee. I usually use that powdered creamer, the French vanilla kind? My neighbor Sarah was telling me I shouldn't drink that stuff because of the preservatives, but she's always on some new health kick. Last month it was juicing celery. I tried to tell her celery doesn't even have any flavor, but she wouldn't listen. She bought this expensive machine and everything. \n\nBut anyway... other days, instead of just the toast, I just find myself standing in the bakery aisle at the grocery store buying all these sweets I don't even really enjoy. They moved the bakery section at my local store right next to the greeting cards, which is so strange to me. I spent twenty minutes looking for a birthday card for my nephew the other day—he just turned fourteen, they grow up so fast—and I ended up with a cart full of muffins and cookies I didn't even want to eat. So, I don't know, my eating is just all over the place lately.\n\nDoctor: How often do you find yourself buying and consuming large amounts of food, even when you don't really want to eat it?\n\nPatient: Well, it's... it's happening a lot more than it used to. Like I said, my eating is just all turned around for months now. Some days I just pick at that toast and coffee, and then other days I'm just standing there in the bakery aisle buying all those sweets I don't even really enjoy. It's probably happening a couple times a week now. Which reminds me, I really need to stop going to that specific grocery store. The lighting in there is just awful, fluorescent lights always give me such a headache. My sister used to work in an office with those same lights and she had to get special glasses just to look at her computer screen. She works in real estate now, doing much better. But yeah, the sweets... I buy them, and then I just eat them without even tasting them, really. It's like I'm not even there.\n\nDoctor: When you find yourself buying and eating those sweets, do you feel like you're not in control of how much you're eating, like you can't stop once you start?\n\nPatient: No, I wouldn't really say it's like that. I don't feel out of control, exactly. It's not like I can't stop. Speaking of stopping, my ex-husband tried to stop smoking a few years back. He used those little patches? They left these awful red welts all over his shoulders. He said they itched terribly. We had to go see a dermatologist, Dr. Evans, who is just the nicest man. He has this beautiful saltwater aquarium right in his waiting room. I could sit and watch those little yellow fish all day long, it's so relaxing. Much better than staring at old magazines from three years ago, you know? They always have those ancient issues of Better Homes and Gardens. \n\nBut anyway... no, with the muffins and things, I could stop if I really wanted to. I just kind of eat them because they're sitting right there on the kitchen counter. I just don't really think about it while I'm doing it.\n\nDoctor: After you eat those sweets, do you feel guilty or upset with yourself about it?\n\nPatient: Guilty? No, not really. I don't get upset about it. It's just... they're just muffins, you know? Though, speaking of getting upset, my neighbor Sarah—the one with the celery juicer—she got so upset the other day because the neighborhood association sent her a letter about her garden hose. They said it was an \"eyesore\" because she left it coiled up next to the porch instead of hiding it behind the bushes. Can you believe that? An eyesore! She was practically in tears over it. I told her, I said, \"Sarah, it's a green hose on green grass, who is even looking at it?\" But she went out and bought this fancy decorative pot just to hide the hose in. It looks like a giant copper cauldron. Every time I look out my kitchen window now, I feel like she's brewing potions over there. \n\nBut no, I don't feel guilty about the sweets. I just eat them and then throw the boxes away. They do take up a lot of room in the recycling bin, though.\n\nDoctor: When you eat those sweets on these days, would you say you're eating a larger amount than most people would eat in that same amount of time?\n\nPatient: Well, I don't know if it's more than most people. I mean, what does a normal person eat, anyway? It's usually just a couple of those big muffins, maybe a sleeve of cookies. It doesn't seem like a mountain of food or anything. Speaking of mountains, did I ever tell you about the time my cousin took a trip to the Rockies? He sent back these postcards that were just stunning. Snow on the peaks in July! He said the air up there is so thin you get winded just walking to the car. He brought me back this little snow globe with a moose inside, but the water leaked out of it on the flight back. It's sitting on my dresser right now, just a dry plastic moose looking confused. It always makes me think of those poor animals in the zoo when it gets too hot. \n\nBut yeah, the sweets... I don't think it's a huge amount. Just whatever's in the box, really.\n\nDoctor: When you're eating those sweets, do you find yourself eating much more quickly than usual?\n\nPatient: Quickly? No, not really. I just eat them at a normal speed, I suppose. I don't see the point in rushing. \n\nEveryone is in such a terrible hurry these days, don't you think? Like our regular mail carrier, Gary. He practically sprints from his little truck up to the porches. Just last week, he was going so fast he tripped right over the garden gnome I have sitting by the bottom step—the one holding the little solar lantern? He knocked it clean over and chipped its red hat. I felt awful for him, but honestly, if he would just slow down a bit... He dropped half my mail right into the rhododendron bush. I was fishing out damp grocery store coupons for three days because it rained that Tuesday. The blue ink got all over my fingers, it was a mess to wash off. \n\nBut anyway... no, I'm not racing anybody to finish a cookie or a muffin. I just chew them normally.\n\nDoctor: Do you usually eat those sweets even when you're not physically hungry?\n\nPatient: Hungry? I don't know, I suppose I must be at least a little hungry when I eat them. I don't really pay much attention to my stomach rumbling or anything like that. \n\nBut speaking of being hungry, you know how they always tell you never to go grocery shopping on an empty stomach? Well, they are absolutely right about that. I went to the store right before lunch on Thursday, and I ended up buying three jars of pickled asparagus. I do not even like asparagus! But the jars just looked so tall and elegant sitting there on the shelf under those bright lights. The cashier, a young kid with a name tag that said Kevin, had to call over a manager because one of the jars would not scan. We stood there for ten minutes while the manager fiddled with the register tape. The line behind me was getting so long, people were shifting from foot to foot, and I felt just terrible. I ended up giving two of the jars to Sarah next door. She said she might try blending them in that juicer of hers. Blended pickled asparagus... it sounds like pond water, doesn't it? \n\nAnyway, what was I saying? Oh, right. I guess I just eat the muffins if I feel like having a snack, so I must be a little hungry for them.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself eating until you feel uncomfortably full when you have these episodes with the sweets?\n\nPatient: Uncomfortably full? No, I don't think so. I mean, I might feel a little full, like you do after a meal, but not sick or anything. I don't really gorge myself until I can't move. \n\nMy uncle Arthur used to do that, though, especially at Thanksgiving. He would eat so much turkey and stuffing that he'd have to unbutton the top of his trousers right there at the table. He always sat in this big recliner we had in the den afterward, and within ten minutes, he'd be snoring so loudly you could hear it in the kitchen over the sound of the dishwasher. My aunt would get so embarrassed, she'd try to wake him up by dropping a magazine on the floor near his head, but he'd just snort and go right back to sleep. They had this beautiful old house with a wraparound porch, and in the summer, they'd sit out there on a porch swing that squeaked every time it went back and forth. I always meant to bring over some WD-40 for that squeak, but I never remembered. I keep a can of it under my kitchen sink, actually, right next to the glass cleaner. You never know when a hinge is going to start acting up. \n\nBut no, I don't eat until I'm bursting. Just until I'm done, I guess.\n\nDoctor: Can you think of a time when you were eating those sweets—was there ever a point where you ate a lot in a short amount of time, like within an hour or two, even if it wasn’t a huge amount overall?\n\nPatient: An hour or two? No, I don't think I've ever just sat there and eaten a whole bunch of them in a short time like that. I don't really watch the clock when I'm having a snack. \n\nAlthough, speaking of clocks, I have this beautiful antique grandfather clock in my front hallway. It belonged to my grandmother, actually. It chimes on the hour, but lately, it’s been running about fifteen minutes fast. I tried to adjust the pendulum myself last week, but the little brass weight is so finicky. I ended up calling a repairman, an older gentleman named Mr. Henderson. He came over on Tuesday and spent three hours just looking at the gears with this tiny little magnifying glass attached to his glasses. He smelled very strongly of peppermint and pipe tobacco, which completely reminded me of my old high school history teacher, Mr. Davies. He used to smoke a pipe right outside the classroom window before the bell rang. Can you imagine a teacher doing that these days? The school board would have an absolute fit! They are so strict now about everything. \n\nBut anyway... what were we talking about? Oh, the sweets. No, I don't eat a lot of them all at once. Just one or two with my tea, usually.\n\nDoctor: After you eat those sweets, do you ever make yourself vomit, take laxatives, or exercise a lot to try to balance it out?\n\nPatient: Vomit? Oh, goodness, no. I could never do that. Just the thought of it makes my stomach turn. And I definitely don't exercise a lot. I mean, I walk to the mailbox, but that's about it. \n\nYou know, speaking of the mailbox, my mail carrier, Diane, she just started taking these aerobics classes at the community center down the road. She was telling me all about it on Wednesday when she dropped off a package for me. It was a new set of those microfiber dish towels I ordered from a catalog. Anyway, Diane said the instructor at this class makes them do these things called burpees? It sounded absolutely exhausting. She was wearing these bright neon pink sneakers, too, the kind that practically glow in the dark. I haven't seen shoes like that since the eighties. I remember I had a pair of leg warmers that were exactly that color. I wore them to a jazzercise class I took with my friend Linda back in 1985. Linda was always trying the newest fitness fads. She even bought one of those stationary bikes with the big fan wheel on the front, but she ended up just using it to hang her damp laundry on in the winter. It actually worked quite well for drying sweaters without stretching them out. \n\nBut no, I don't do anything like that after I have a muffin. I just eat it and go about my day.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever eat those sweets by yourself when no one else is around, or try to hide that you’ve eaten them from others?\n\nPatient: Hide them? No, I don't really hide them. I mean, I live alone, so there's not really anyone here to hide them from anyway. It's just me and the house. \n\nThough, my neighbor, Mrs. Gable, she's always popping over unannounced. She has this habit of just walking right through the back door if it's unlocked. She did it just last week, actually, to bring over some tomatoes from her garden. They were those big, ugly heirloom ones, but they tasted wonderful. Anyway, she startled me so badly I nearly dropped my teacup. She’s a sweet woman, but she talks a mile a minute, mostly about her grandson who’s away at college in Ohio. He's studying to be an engineer, I think. Or maybe an architect? I always get those two mixed up. He used to mow my lawn in the summers before he left. He did a terrible job around the edges, always missed the spot near the rhododendron bush, but I never had the heart to tell him. It’s hard to find reliable help these days. I tried looking in the local paper for a new lawn service, but the print is so small now, I can barely read it even with my reading glasses. I think I need a new prescription. \n\nBut no, I don't hide my sweets. If I want a cookie, I just have one.\n\nDoctor: Can you tell me if there are times when you eat a lot in a short amount of time, like over the course of a few hours, even if it doesn’t feel like you’re out of control while you're doing it?\n\nPatient: No, I really don't think so. I don't sit there for hours just eating and eating. I might have a little something, but not a lot all at once. \n\nAlthough, speaking of eating for hours, my cousin Barbara used to host these Thanksgiving dinners that felt like they went on for days. She lived in this huge Victorian house out in the country, and she would cook for a week beforehand. She always made this sweet potato casserole with the little marshmallows on top, but she would burn them just a tiny bit, which I actually prefer. Anyway, one year, her husband, Frank, decided he was going to deep-fry the turkey in the driveway. He bought this massive contraption, it looked like an oil drum. Well, he didn't measure the oil right or something, and the whole thing bubbled over and caught fire! It was a disaster. The fire department had to come, and the big red truck got stuck in the mud at the end of her driveway because it had rained for three days straight before that. The firemen were very nice about it, though. One of them, a young man with bright red hair, even stayed and had a plate of the sweet potatoes after they put the fire out. He said it reminded him of his grandmother's cooking. I haven't seen Barbara in years, though. We had a falling out over a quilt that belonged to our aunt. It's silly, really, how families fight over things like that. \n\nBut what were you asking? Oh, eating a lot in a few hours. No, I don't do that. Just a normal amount, I'd say.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever eaten a lot of food when you were alone because you felt embarrassed about how much you were eating or what you were eating?\n\nPatient: Embarrassed? No, I don't think I've ever felt embarrassed about eating. Like I said, I live alone, so it's not like there's anyone here to judge me anyway. If I want a piece of cake, I just have a piece of cake. \n\nYou know, talking about cake, the bakery down on Main Street—the one with the green awning, not the one by the post office—they make the most incredible lemon pound cake. I used to go there every Tuesday morning with my friend Clara. We’d sit in the window booth and watch the people walk by. Clara always wore these big, floppy hats, even in the dead of winter. She said they protected her skin, but I think she just liked the attention. She had a hat for every occasion. I remember she wore a bright yellow one with a sunflower on it to a funeral once. It was her great-uncle's funeral, and he loved gardening, so she said it was a tribute, but I think some of the other relatives were a bit put off by it. They were a very traditional family, the kind that only wore black and whispered the whole time. I always preferred a celebration of life, myself. When my cat, Mr. Whiskers, passed away, I invited a few neighbors over and we shared stories about him. He was a tabby, very independent, but he loved to sleep on top of the refrigerator. \n\nBut anyway, no, I don't hide my food or feel embarrassed about it. I just eat what I want when I want it.\n\nDoctor: Can you tell me if there are times when you eat a much larger amount of food than you would normally eat in one sitting, even if it doesn’t feel like a huge amount at the time?\n\nPatient: No, I wouldn't say I eat a much larger amount than normal, really. I just have my regular meals, maybe a snack here and there. I don't really have a big appetite anyway. \n\nMy appetite hasn't been the same since they closed down that diner on 4th Street. The Starlight Diner, it was called. They had these red vinyl booths that would stick to the back of your legs in the summer if you wore shorts. I used to go there for meatloaf on Thursdays. The waitress, Brenda, she always knew exactly how much gravy I liked. She had this big hair, you know, the kind they teased up real high in the eighties, and she always wore this bright pink lipstick that would leave a mark on the coffee cups. She told me once she bought it in bulk from some catalog because they were going to discontinue the color. \"Flamingo Sunrise,\" I think it was called. Or maybe \"Coral Sunset\"? Anyway, she had a daughter who was a competitive ice skater. She was always showing me pictures of her in these little sequined outfits. I think she made it to the regionals one year, but then she twisted her ankle doing a jump. It's a shame, really, she was very talented. I used to ice skate when I was a girl, out on the pond behind my uncle's farm, but the ice was never very smooth. You really had to watch out for the bumps or you'd go flying. \n\nBut no, I don't eat huge amounts of food in one sitting. Just my normal portions.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever eat alone because you're worried others might judge what or how much you're eating?\n\nPatient: No, I don't really worry about people judging me. I mostly eat alone anyway, but that's just because I live by myself. It's not because I'm hiding anything. \n\nSpeaking of eating alone, I used to have this little parakeet named Pip. He was bright blue, like a summer sky, and he would sit on my shoulder while I ate my breakfast. He loved toast crumbs. I got him at a pet store that used to be over by the old mall. It's a shame, really, because the town could use something fun for the kids. When I was a kid, we used to go to the roller rink on Friday nights. They played disco music and had a disco ball that scattered little squares of light all over the floor. I had these white skates with pink pom-poms on the toes. I thought I was just the bee's knees. \n\nBut what were we talking about? Oh, eating alone. No, I don't hide from people to eat. He said the air up there is so thin you get winded just walking to the car. He brought me back this little snow globe with a moose inside, but the water leaked out of it on the flight back. It's sitting on my dresser right now, just a dry plastic moose looking confused. It always makes me think of those poor animals in the zoo when it gets too hot. \n\nBut yeah, the sweets... I don't think it's a huge amount. Just whatever's in the box, really.\n\nDoctor: When you're eating those sweets, do you find yourself eating much more quickly than usual?\n\nPatient: Quickly? No, not really. I just eat them at a normal speed, I suppose. I don't see the point in rushing. \n\nEveryone is in such a terrible hurry these days, don't you think? Like our regular mail carrier, Gary. He practically sprints from his little truck up to the porches. Just last week, he was going so fast he tripped right over the garden gnome I have sitting by the bottom step—the one holding the little solar lantern? He knocked it clean over and chipped its red hat. I felt awful for him, but honestly, if he would just slow down a bit... He dropped half my mail right into the rhododendron bush. I was fishing out damp grocery store coupons for three days because it rained that Tuesday. The blue ink got all over my fingers, it was a mess to wash off. \n\nBut anyway... no, I'm not racing anybody to finish a cookie or a muffin. I just chew them normally.\n\nDoctor: Do you usually eat those sweets even when you're not physically hungry?\n\nPatient: Hungry? I don't know, I suppose I must be at least a little hungry when I eat them. I don't really pay much attention to my stomach rumbling or anything like that. \n\nBut speaking of being hungry, you know how they always tell you never to go grocery shopping on an empty stomach? Well, they are absolutely right about that. I went to the store right before lunch on Thursday, and I ended up buying three jars of pickled asparagus. I do not even like asparagus! But the jars just looked so tall and elegant sitting there on the shelf under those bright lights. The cashier, a young kid with a name tag that said Kevin, had to call over a manager because one of the jars would not scan. We stood there for ten minutes while the manager fiddled with the register tape. The line behind me was getting so long, people were shifting from foot to foot, and I felt just terrible. I ended up giving two of the jars to Sarah next door. She said she might try blending them in that juicer of hers. Blended pickled asparagus... it sounds like pond water, doesn't it? \n\nAnyway, what was I saying? Oh, right. I guess I just eat the muffins if I feel like having a snack, so I must be a little hungry for them.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself eating until you feel uncomfortably full when you have these episodes with the sweets?\n\nPatient: Uncomfortably full? No, I don't think so. I mean, I might feel a little full, like you do after a meal, but not sick or anything. I don't really gorge myself until I can't move. \n\nMy uncle Arthur used to do that, though, especially at Thanksgiving. He would eat so much turkey and stuffing that he'd have to unbutton the top of his trousers right there at the table. He always sat in this big recliner we had in the den afterward, and within ten minutes, he'd be snoring so loudly you could hear it in the kitchen over the sound of the dishwasher. My aunt would get so embarrassed, she'd try to wake him up by dropping a magazine on the floor near his head, but he'd just snort and go right back to sleep. They had this beautiful old house with a wraparound porch, and in the summer, they'd sit out there on a porch swing that squeaked every time it went back and forth. I always meant to bring over some WD-40 for that squeak, but I never remembered. I keep a can of it under my kitchen sink, actually, right next to the glass cleaner. You never know when a hinge is going to start acting up. \n\nBut no, I don't eat until I'm bursting. Just until I'm done, I guess.\n\nDoctor: Can you think of a time when you were eating those sweets—was there ever a point where you ate a lot in a short amount of time, like within an hour or two, even if it wasn’t a huge amount overall?\n\nPatient: An hour or two? No, I don't think I've ever just sat there and eaten a whole bunch of them in a short time like that. I don't really watch the clock when I'm having a snack. \n\nAlthough, speaking of clocks, I have this beautiful antique grandfather clock in my front hallway. It belonged to my grandmother, actually. It chimes on the hour, but lately, it’s been running about fifteen minutes fast. I tried to adjust the pendulum myself last week, but the little brass weight is so finicky. I ended up calling a repairman, an older gentleman named Mr. Henderson. He came over on Tuesday and spent three hours just looking at the gears with this tiny little magnifying glass attached to his glasses. He smelled very strongly of peppermint and pipe tobacco, which completely reminded me of my old high school history teacher, Mr. Davies. He used to smoke a pipe right outside the classroom window before the bell rang. Can you imagine a teacher doing that these days? The school board would have an absolute fit! They are so strict now about everything. \n\nBut anyway... what were we talking about? Oh, the sweets. No, I don't eat a lot of them all at once. Just one or two with my tea, usually.\n\nDoctor: After you eat those sweets, do you ever make yourself vomit, take laxatives, or exercise a lot to try to balance it out?\n\nPatient: Vomit? Oh, goodness, no. I could never do that. Just the thought of it makes my stomach turn. And I definitely don't exercise a lot. I mean, I walk to the mailbox, but that's about it. \n\nYou know, speaking of the mailbox, my mail carrier, Diane, she just started taking these aerobics classes at the community center down the road. She was telling me all about it on Wednesday when she dropped off a package for me. It was a new set of those microfiber dish towels I ordered from a catalog. Anyway, Diane said the instructor at this class makes them do these things called burpees? It sounded absolutely exhausting. She was wearing these bright neon pink sneakers, too, the kind that practically glow in the dark. I haven't seen shoes like that since the eighties. I remember I had a pair of leg warmers that were exactly that color. I wore them to a jazzercise class I took with my friend Linda back in 1985. Linda was always trying the newest fitness fads. She even bought one of those stationary bikes with the big fan wheel on the front, but she ended up just using it to hang her damp laundry on in the winter. It actually worked quite well for drying sweaters without stretching them out. \n\nBut no, I don't do anything like that after I have a muffin. I just eat it and go about my day.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever eat those sweets by yourself when no one else is around, or try to hide that you’ve eaten them from others?\n\nPatient: Hide them? No, I don't really hide them. I mean, I live alone, so there's not really anyone here to hide them from anyway. It's just me and the house. \n\nThough, my neighbor, Mrs. Gable, she's always popping over unannounced. She has this habit of just walking right through the back door if it's unlocked. She did it just last week, actually, to bring over some tomatoes from her garden. They were those big, ugly heirloom ones, but they tasted wonderful. Anyway, she startled me so badly I nearly dropped my teacup. She’s a sweet woman, but she talks a mile a minute, mostly about her grandson who’s away at college in Ohio. He's studying to be an engineer, I think. Or maybe an architect? I always get those two mixed up. He used to mow my lawn in the summers before he left. He did a terrible job around the edges, always missed the spot near the rhododendron bush, but I never had the heart to tell him. It’s hard to find reliable help these days. I tried looking in the local paper for a new lawn service, but the print is so small now, I can barely read it even with my reading glasses. I think I need a new prescription. \n\nBut no, I don't hide my sweets. If I want a cookie, I just have one.\n\nDoctor: Can you tell me if there are times when you eat a lot in a short amount of time, like over the course of a few hours, even if it doesn’t feel like you’re out of control while you're doing it?\n\nPatient: No, I really don't think so. I don't sit there for hours just eating and eating. I might have a little something, but not a lot all at once. \n\nAlthough, speaking of eating for hours, my cousin Barbara used to host these Thanksgiving dinners that felt like they went on for days. She lived in this huge Victorian house out in the country, and she would cook for a week beforehand. She always made this sweet potato casserole with the little marshmallows on top, but she would burn them just a tiny bit, which I actually prefer. Anyway, one year, her husband, Frank, decided he was going to deep-fry the turkey in the driveway. He bought this massive contraption, it looked like an oil drum. Well, he didn't measure the oil right or something, and the whole thing bubbled over and caught fire! It was a disaster. The fire department had to come, and the big red truck got stuck in the mud at the end of her driveway because it had rained for three days straight before that. The firemen were very nice about it, though. One of them, a young man with bright red hair, even stayed and had a plate of the sweet potatoes after they put the fire out. He said it reminded him of his grandmother's cooking. I haven't seen Barbara in years, though. We had a falling out over a quilt that belonged to our aunt. It's silly, really, how families fight over things like that. \n\nBut what were you asking? Oh, eating a lot in a few hours. No, I don't do that. Just a normal amount, I'd say.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever eaten a lot of food when you were alone because you felt embarrassed about how much you were eating or what you were eating?\n\nPatient: Embarrassed? No, I don't think I've ever felt embarrassed about eating. Like I said, I live alone, so it's not like there's anyone here to judge me anyway. If I want a piece of cake, I just have a piece of cake. \n\nYou know, talking about cake, the bakery down on Main Street—the one with the green awning, not the one by the post office—they make the most incredible lemon pound cake. I used to go there every Tuesday morning with my friend Clara. We’d sit in the window booth and watch the people walk by. Clara always wore these big, floppy hats, even in the dead of winter. She said they protected her skin, but I think she just liked the attention. She had a hat for every occasion. I remember she wore a bright yellow one with a sunflower on it to a funeral once. It was her great-uncle's funeral, and he loved gardening, so she said it was a tribute, but I think some of the other relatives were a bit put off by it. They were a very traditional family, the kind that only wore black and whispered the whole time. I always preferred a celebration of life, myself. When my cat, Mr. Whiskers, passed away, I invited a few neighbors over and we shared stories about him. He was a tabby, very independent, but he loved to sleep on top of the refrigerator. \n\nBut anyway, no, I don't hide my food or feel embarrassed about it. I just eat what I want when I want it.\n\nDoctor: Can you tell me if there are times when you eat a much larger amount of food than you would normally eat in one sitting, even if it doesn’t feel like a huge amount at the time?\n\nPatient: No, I wouldn't say I eat a much larger amount than normal, really. I just have my regular meals, maybe a snack here and there. I don't really have a big appetite anyway. \n\nMy appetite hasn't been the same since they closed down that diner on 4th Street. The Starlight Diner, it was called. They had these red vinyl booths that would stick to the back of your legs in the summer if you wore shorts. I used to go there for meatloaf on Thursdays. The waitress, Brenda, she always knew exactly how much gravy I liked. She had this big hair, you know, the kind they teased up real high in the eighties, and she always wore this bright pink lipstick that would leave a mark on the coffee cups. She told me once she bought it in bulk from some catalog because they were going to discontinue the color. \"Flamingo Sunrise,\" I think it was called. Or maybe \"Coral Sunset\"? Anyway, she had a daughter who was a competitive ice skater. She was always showing me pictures of her in these little sequined outfits. I think she made it to the regionals one year, but then she twisted her ankle doing a jump. It's a shame, really, she was very talented. I used to ice skate when I was a girl, out on the pond behind my uncle's farm, but the ice was never very smooth. You really had to watch out for the bumps or you'd go flying. \n\nBut no, I don't eat huge amounts of food in one sitting. Just my normal portions.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever eat alone because you're worried others might judge what or how much you're eating?\n\nPatient: No, I don't really worry about people judging me. I mostly eat alone anyway, but that's just because I live by myself. It's not because I'm hiding anything. \n\nSpeaking of eating alone, I used to have this little parakeet named Pip. He was bright blue, like a summer sky, and he would sit on my shoulder while I ate my breakfast. He loved toast crumbs. I got him at a pet store that used to be over by the old mall. That mall is completely empty now, just a big concrete eyesore. They were supposed to turn it into an indoor water park or something, but the company ran out of money. It's a shame, really, because the town could use something fun for the kids. When I was a kid, we used to go to the roller rink on Friday nights. They played disco music and had a disco ball that scattered little squares of light all over the floor. I had these white skates with pink pom-poms on the toes. I thought I was just the bee's knees. \n\nBut what were we talking about? Oh, eating alone. No, I don't hide from people to eat. I just eat my meals.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
No, no, I don't hide my eating. I don't really have anything to be ashamed of. I just eat my regular meals, like I said. 

Although, speaking of secrets... the television has been trying to tell me things lately. Not out loud, but in the static. Between the channels. If you listen really closely, you can hear them whispering about the neighbors. The neighbors across the street, the ones with the red door. They painted it last spring, and I remember thinking it was such a bold choice. Red is a very aggressive color, you know. My mother had a red dress she wore to holiday parties. She always smelled like peppermint and hairspray. But the static... it says the neighbors are watching me. I don't know why they would. I'm just a quiet person. I keep to myself. But the television knows. 

But no, I don't eat in secret. I just eat my food.

Like, some days I just sit and pick at a piece of dry toast and have some coffee. I usually use that powdered creamer, the French vanilla kind? My neighbor Sarah was telling me I shouldn't drink that stuff because of the preservatives, but she's always on some new health kick. Last month it was juicing celery. Like I said, my eating is just all turned around for months now. Some days I just pick at that toast and coffee, and then other days I'm just standing there in the bakery aisle buying all those sweets I don't even really enjoy. It's probably happening a couple times a week now. Which reminds me, I really need to stop going to that specific grocery store. The lighting in there is just awful, fluorescent lights always give me such a headache. My sister used to work in an office with those same lights and she had to get special glasses just to look at her computer screen. She works in real estate now, doing much better. But yeah, the sweets... I buy them, and then I just eat them without even tasting them, really. It's like I'm not even there.\n\nDoctor: When you find yourself buying and eating those sweets, do you feel like you're not in control of how much you're eating, like you can't stop once you start?\n\nPatient: No, I wouldn't really say it's like that. I don't feel out of control, exactly. It's not like I can't stop. Speaking of stopping, my ex-husband tried to stop smoking a few years back. He used those little patches? They left these awful red welts all over his shoulders. He said they itched terribly. We had to go see a dermatologist, Dr. Evans, who is just the nicest man. He has this beautiful saltwater aquarium right in his waiting room. I could sit and watch those little yellow fish all day long, it's so relaxing. Much better than staring at old magazines from three years ago, you know? They always have those ancient issues of Better Homes and Gardens. \n\nBut anyway... no, with the muffins and things, I could stop if I really wanted to. I just kind of eat them because they're sitting right there on the kitchen counter. I just don't really think about it while I'm doing it.\n\nDoctor: After you eat those sweets, do you feel guilty or upset with yourself about it?\n\nPatient: Guilty? No, not really. I don't get upset about it. It's just... they're just muffins, you know? Though, speaking of getting upset, my neighbor Sarah—the one with the celery juicer—she got so upset the other day because the neighborhood association sent her a letter about her garden hose. They said it was an \"eyesore\" because she left it coiled up next to the porch instead of hiding it behind the bushes. Can you believe that? An eyesore! She was practically in tears over it. I told her, I said, \"Sarah, it's a green hose on green grass, who is even looking at it?\" But she went out and bought this fancy decorative pot just to hide the hose in. It looks like a giant copper cauldron. Every time I look out my kitchen window now, I feel like she's brewing potions over there. \n\nBut no, I don't feel guilty about the sweets. I just eat them and then throw the boxes away. They do take up a lot of room in the recycling bin, though.\n\nDoctor: When you eat those sweets on these days, would you say you're eating a larger amount than most people would eat in that same amount of time?\n\nPatient: Well, I don't know if it's more than most people. I mean, what does a normal person eat, anyway? It's usually just a couple of those big muffins, maybe a sleeve of cookies. It doesn't seem like a mountain of food or anything. Speaking of mountains, did I ever tell you about the time my cousin took a trip to the Rockies? He sent back these postcards that were just stunning. Snow on the peaks in July! He said the air up there is so thin you get winded just walking to the car. He brought me back this little snow globe with a moose inside, but the water leaked out of it on the flight back. It's sitting on my dresser right now, just a dry plastic moose looking confused. It always makes me think of those poor animals in the zoo when it gets too hot. \n\nBut yeah, the sweets... I don't think it's a huge amount. Just whatever's in the box, really.\n\nDoctor: When you're eating those sweets, do you find yourself eating much more quickly than usual?\n\nPatient: Quickly? No, not really. I just eat them at a normal speed, I suppose. I don't see the point in rushing. \n\nEveryone is in such a terrible hurry these days, don't you think? Like our regular mail carrier, Gary. He practically sprints from his little truck up to the porches. Just last week, he was going so fast he tripped right over the garden gnome I have sitting by the bottom step—the one holding the little solar lantern? He knocked it clean over and chipped its red hat. I felt awful for him, but honestly, if he would just slow down a bit... He dropped half my mail right into the rhododendron bush. I was fishing out damp grocery store coupons for three days because it rained that Tuesday. The blue ink got all over my fingers, it was a mess to wash off. \n\nBut anyway... no, I'm not racing anybody to finish a cookie or a muffin. I just chew them normally.\n\nDoctor: Do you usually eat those sweets even when you're not physically hungry?\n\nPatient: Hungry? I don't know, I suppose I must be at least a little hungry when I eat them. I don't really pay much attention to my stomach rumbling or anything like that. \n\nBut speaking of being hungry, you know how they always tell you never to go grocery shopping on an empty stomach? Well, they are absolutely right about that. I went to the store right before lunch on Thursday, and I ended up buying three jars of pickled asparagus. I do not even like asparagus! But the jars just looked so tall and elegant sitting there on the shelf under those bright lights. The cashier, a young kid with a name tag that said Kevin, had to call over a manager because one of the jars would not scan. We stood there for ten minutes while the manager fiddled with the register tape. The line behind me was getting so long, people were shifting from foot to foot, and I felt just terrible. I ended up giving two of the jars to Sarah next door. She said she might try blending them in that juicer of hers. Blended pickled asparagus... it sounds like pond water, doesn't it? \n\nAnyway, what was I saying? Oh, right. I guess I just eat the muffins if I feel like having a snack, so I must be a little hungry for them.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself eating until you feel uncomfortably full when you have these episodes with the sweets?\n\nPatient: Uncomfortably full? No, I don't think so. I mean, I might feel a little full, like you do after a meal, but not sick or anything. I don't really gorge myself until I can't move. \n\nMy uncle Arthur used to do that, though, especially at Thanksgiving. He would eat so much turkey and stuffing that he'd have to unbutton the top of his trousers right there at the table. He always sat in this big recliner we had in the den afterward, and within ten minutes, he'd be snoring so loudly you could hear it in the kitchen over the sound of the dishwasher. My aunt would get so embarrassed, she'd try to wake him up by dropping a magazine on the floor near his head, but he'd just snort and go right back to sleep. They had this beautiful old house with a wraparound porch, and in the summer, they'd sit out there on a porch swing that squeaked every time it went back and forth. I always meant to bring over some WD-40 for that squeak, but I never remembered. I keep a can of it under my kitchen sink, actually, right next to the glass cleaner. You never know when a hinge is going to start acting up. \n\nBut no, I don't eat until I'm bursting. Just until I'm done, I guess.\n\nDoctor: Can you think of a time when you were eating those sweets—was there ever a point where you ate a lot in a short amount of time, like within an hour or two, even if it wasn’t a huge amount overall?\n\nPatient: An hour or two? No, I don't think I've ever just sat there and eaten a whole bunch of them in a short time like that. I don't really watch the clock when I'm having a snack. \n\nAlthough, speaking of clocks, I have this beautiful antique grandfather clock in my front hallway. It belonged to my grandmother, actually. It chimes on the hour, but lately, it’s been running about fifteen minutes fast. I tried to adjust the pendulum myself last week, but the little brass weight is so finicky. I ended up calling a repairman, an older gentleman named Mr. Henderson. He came over on Tuesday and spent three hours just looking at the gears with this tiny little magnifying glass attached to his glasses. He smelled very strongly of peppermint and pipe tobacco, which completely reminded me of my old high school history teacher, Mr. Davies. He used to smoke a pipe right outside the classroom window before the bell rang. Can you imagine a teacher doing that these days? The school board would have an absolute fit! They are so strict now about everything. \n\nBut anyway... what were we talking about? Oh, the sweets. No, I don't eat a lot of them all at once. Just one or two with my tea, usually.\n\nDoctor: After you eat those sweets, do you ever make yourself vomit, take laxatives, or exercise a lot to try to balance it out?\n\nPatient: Vomit? Oh, goodness, no. I could never do that. Just the thought of it makes my stomach turn. And I definitely don't exercise a lot. I mean, I walk to the mailbox, but that's about it. \n\nYou know, speaking of the mailbox, my mail carrier, Diane, she just started taking these aerobics classes at the community center down the road. She was telling me all about it on Wednesday when she dropped off a package for me. It was a new set of those microfiber dish towels I ordered from a catalog. Anyway, Diane said the instructor at this class makes them do these things called burpees? It sounded absolutely exhausting. She even bought one of those stationary bikes with the big fan wheel on the front, but she ended up just using it to hang her damp laundry on in the winter. It actually worked quite well for drying sweaters without stretching them out. \n\nBut no, I don't do anything like that after I have a muffin. I just eat it and go about my day.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever eat those sweets by yourself when no one else is around, or try to hide that you’ve eaten them from others?\n\nPatient: Hide them? No, I don't really hide them. I mean, I live alone, so there's not really anyone here to hide them from anyway. It's just me and the house. \n\nThough, my neighbor, Mrs. Gable, she's always popping over unannounced. She has this habit of just walking right through the back door if it's unlocked. She did it just last week, actually, to bring over some tomatoes from her garden. They were those big, ugly heirloom ones, but they tasted wonderful. Anyway, she startled me so badly I nearly dropped my teacup. She’s a sweet woman, but she talks a mile a minute, mostly about her grandson who’s away at college in Ohio. He's studying to be an engineer, I think. Or maybe an architect? I always get those two mixed up. He used to mow my lawn in the summers before he left. He did a terrible job around the edges, always missed the spot near the rhododendron bush, but I never had the heart to tell him. It’s hard to find reliable help these days. I tried looking in the local paper for a new lawn service, but the print is so small now, I can barely read it even with my reading glasses. I think I need a new prescription. \n\nBut no, I don't hide my sweets. If I want a cookie, I just have one.\n\nDoctor: Can you tell me if there are times when you eat a lot in a short amount of time, like over the course of a few hours, even if it doesn’t feel like you’re out of control while you're doing it?\n\nPatient: No, I really don't think so. I don't sit there for hours just eating and eating. I might have a little something, but not a lot all at once. \n\nAlthough, speaking of eating for hours, my cousin Barbara used to host these Thanksgiving dinners that felt like they went on for days. She lived in this huge Victorian house out in the country, and she would cook for a week beforehand. She always made this sweet potato casserole with the little marshmallows on top, but she would burn them just a tiny bit, which I actually prefer. Anyway, one year, her husband, Frank, decided he was going to deep-fry the turkey in the driveway. He bought this massive contraption, it looked like an oil drum. Well, he didn't measure the oil right or something, and the whole thing bubbled over and caught fire! It was a disaster. The fire department had to come, and the big red truck got stuck in the mud at the end of her driveway because it had rained for three days straight before that. The firemen were very nice about it, though. One of them, a young man with bright red hair, even stayed and had a plate of the sweet potatoes after they put the fire out. He said it reminded him of his grandmother's cooking. I haven't seen Barbara in years, though. We had a falling out over a quilt that belonged to our aunt. It's silly, really, how families fight over things like that. \n\nBut what were you asking? Oh, eating a lot in a few hours. No, I don't do that. Just a normal amount, I'd say.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever eaten a lot of food when you were alone because you felt embarrassed about how much you were eating or what you were eating?\n\nPatient: Embarrassed? No, I don't think I've ever felt embarrassed about eating. Like I said, I live alone, so it's not like there's anyone here to judge me anyway. If I want a piece of cake, I just have a piece of cake. \n\nYou know, talking about cake, the bakery down on Main Street—the one with the green awning, not the one by the post office—they make the most incredible lemon pound cake. I used to go there every Tuesday morning with my friend Clara. We’d sit in the window booth and watch the people walk by. Clara always wore these big, floppy hats, even in the dead of winter. She said they protected her skin, but I think she just liked the attention. She had a hat for every occasion. I remember she wore a bright yellow one with a sunflower on it to a funeral once. It was her great-uncle's funeral, and he loved gardening, so she said it was a tribute, but I think some of the other relatives were a bit put off by it. They were a very traditional family, the kind that only wore black and whispered the whole time. I always preferred a celebration of life, myself. When my cat, Mr. Whiskers, passed away, I invited a few neighbors over and we shared stories about him. He was a tabby, very independent, but he loved to sleep on top of the refrigerator. \n\nBut anyway, no, I don't hide my food or feel embarrassed about it. I just eat what I want when I want it.\n\nDoctor: Can you tell me if there are times when you eat a much larger amount of food than you would normally eat in one sitting, even if it doesn’t feel like a huge amount at the time?\n\nPatient: No, I wouldn't say I eat a much larger amount than normal, really. I just have my regular meals, maybe a snack here and there. I don't really have a big appetite anyway. \n\nMy appetite hasn't been the same since they closed down that diner on 4th Street. The Starlight Diner, it was called. They had these red vinyl booths that would stick to the back of your legs in the summer if you wore shorts. I used to go there for meatloaf on Thursdays. The waitress, Brenda, she always knew exactly how much gravy I liked. She had this big hair, you know, the kind they teased up real high in the eighties, and she always wore this bright pink lipstick that would leave a mark on the coffee cups. She told me once she bought it in bulk from some catalog because they were going to discontinue the color. \"Flamingo Sunrise,\" I think it was called. Or maybe \"Coral Sunset\"? Anyway, she had a daughter who was a competitive ice skater. She was always showing me pictures of her in these little sequined outfits. I think she made it to the regionals one year, but then she twisted her ankle doing a jump. It's a shame, really, she was very talented. I used to ice skate when I was a girl, out on the pond behind my uncle's farm, but the ice was never very smooth. You really had to watch out for the bumps or you'd go flying. \n\nBut no, I don't eat huge amounts of food in one sitting. Just my normal portions.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever eat alone because you're worried others might judge what or how much you're eating?\n\nPatient: No, I don't really worry about people judging me. I mostly eat alone anyway, but that's just because I live by myself. It's not because I'm hiding anything. \n\nSpeaking of eating alone, I used to have this little parakeet named Pip. He was bright blue, like a summer sky, and he would sit on my shoulder while I ate my breakfast. He loved toast crumbs. I got him at a pet store that used to be over by the old mall. That mall is completely empty now, just a big concrete eyesore. They were supposed to turn it into an indoor water park or something, but the company ran out of money. It's a shame, really, because the town could use something fun for the kids. When I was a kid, we used to go to the roller rink on Friday nights. They played disco music and had a disco ball that scattered little squares of light all over the floor. I had these white skates with pink pom-poms on the toes. I thought I was just the bee's knees. \n\nBut what were we talking about? Oh, eating alone. No, I don't hide from people to eat. I just eat my meals.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever eaten in secret or tried to hide your eating from others because you felt ashamed or embarrassed about what or how much you were eating?\n\nPatient: No, no, I don't hide my eating. I don't really have anything to be ashamed of. I just eat my regular meals, like I said. \n\nAlthough, speaking of secrets... the television has been trying to tell me things lately. Not out loud, but in the static. Between the channels. If you listen really closely, you can hear them whispering about the neighbors. The neighbors across the street, the ones with the red door. They painted it last spring, and I remember thinking it was such a bold choice. Red is a very aggressive color, you know. My mother had a red dress she wore to holiday parties. She always smelled like peppermint and hairspray. But the static... it says the neighbors are watching me. I don't know why they would. I'm just a quiet person. I keep to myself.
